THE AUTHOR. 


Preface to the Second Edition. 





glance at the Table of Contents will show that this edition 
differe from the first in many respects. The additions and 
alterations that have been made both in matter and arrangement 
require a few words of explanation. 


The main body of the work is divided into four parts. The first 
part gives the general scope of Syntaz and lays down the princtpal 
laws of Concord. The second part deals with Government, and gives 
principal rules in the Aarake Prakarana. In the third part are. 
considered the more important Grammatical Forms, the meaning and 
use of which require explanation; such as, several kinds of Participles. 
the Infinitive Mood, the ten Tenses and Moods. Particles, such as are 
most frequently used in Sanskrit Literature, are also treated and 
illustrated, being alphabetically arranged and distributed over eight 
Lessons. Some peculiarities of the Parasmaipada and A’tmanepada— 
certain roots taking the one or the other pada according as they are 
used in a particular sense or are preceded by certain prepositions— 
which were given in an Appendix in the first edition. have here been 
incorporated into the body of the work, and treated iu two additional 
Lessons. The fourth part gives matter not given in the first edition 
—the Analysis and Synthesis of Sanskrit Sentences. I have tried to 
apply the system of English analysis to Sanskrit sentences, and in 
doing so I have illustrated the rules of English Grammar by examples 
from Sanskrit authors, making such additions and alterations as were 
necessitated by the peculiarity of the Sanskrit idiom. To some this 
portion may perhaps appear superfluous. But my experience is that 
a vorrect knowledge of the relations subsisting between the different 
parts of aSanskrit sentence is highly useful to the student. not only 
in translating from Sanskrit into English, but also in translating from 


vi 


English injo Sanskrit, inasmuch as it clearly brings to his notice the 
difference in construction between the two languages, and in composing. 
sentences. The general rules of Analysis are much the same in all 
languages, but their application is not easily understood. In Section 
IL.of this part some rules on the order of words have been given, 
mostly drawn from an examination of the construction of Sanskrit 
Sentences and comparison with Latin idiom. The third Section takes 
up the Composition of sentences, where the student has to frame some 
sentences so as to apply the rules of analysis given in the first Section. 
Several exercises have, with this view, been given in this Section. 1 am 
inclined to believe that these exercises, if carefully worked, will give 
the student considerable facility in writing a few sentences of original 
Sanskrit on a given subject. The student has also been shown how 
to paraphrase Sanskrit passages, and it is expected that, with the help 
of Analysis, he will be able to paraphrase in Sanskrit as he does in 
English. The fourth Section treats of Letter-writting, in which are 
given, with examples aud exercises, some of the common forms of 
letters. On this subject I have derived considerable help froma 
number of manuscripts, dealing with srgrequ:—forms of writing— 
that were brought to my notice by Dr. Bhandarkar, and kindly placed 
at my disposal for some months. 


This edition differs also in the arrangement of matter. Each 
Lesson here consists of three parts: the first gives the rules with 
illustrations; the second and third give sentences for exercise. 
Choice Expressions and Idioms, which were, in the former editions; 
given after the rules, and the Sentences for Correction, which were 
given last, have here been given after the Notes. The Idioms have 
been arranged in the alphabetical order of the important words in 
their English equivalents, anda good many taken from standard 
authors have been added so as to increase the former number by over 
one-half. The Sentencss for Correction have been promiscuously 
arranged, and they may be attempted after the rules have been fully 


vii 


mastered. There is one more material change in arrangement that 
will not fail to strike the reader. The Sanskrit sentences have been 
divided into two parts: those in large type for reading in class, and 
* Additional Sentences for Exercise, which may be read at home as 
additional reading. I have been obliged to make this division, not 
because I considered the number of sentences very large, as some of 
my critics did, but because the sentences, as they stood, were too 
many to be read by students in the ordinary course of class lessons. 
I myself felt the difficulty, while teaching the book; and I thought it 
proper to do that which I myself did, and which other teachers also. 
who did me the honour of teaching it to their pupils, must have done, 
namely to effect a division of the Sanskrit sentences. This has, 
moreover, enabled me to add under the * Additional Sentences? severai 
passages from authors not previously drawn upon. 


The lesson on the Nominative case in the first edition has been 
omitted, as it was found to be superfluous, and that on Pronouns, 
being out of place in Concord, has been transferred to Part IIT. The 
Appendix on the formation of the feminine of nouns and adjectives 
has bcen dropped. 


Other improvements made in this edition are two Glossartes— 
Sanskrit-English and English—Sanskrit—which give the difficult 
words occurring in the exercises for translation, and an alphabetical 
Index of all the nouns, adjectives, roots, &c. which have given rise to 
syntactical or other rules. The want of the two Glossaries, more 
especially of the first, was much felt by students. The most ordinary 
words, which the student must have come across in his elementary 
course of study, have not been included. The importance of the 
Index need not be much dilated upon, since it facilitates reference to 

very remarkable degree and is now regarded as almost & sine qua 
‘non of such works. For this I must thank Professor Max Müller, 
who was kind enough to suggest, among other things, this idea of 
giving an Index. The Notes, given after Part IV., retain much of 


viii 


‘their former character. They are mostly intended to be explanatory. 
Individual words, being included iu the Glossaries, have not here been 
repeated. 

These are the main additions aud alterations made in this edition. 
Besides, the work has been most carefully revised throughout; indeed, 
it will be difficult to find a page which has not undergone careful 
revision, Several rules have beon recast; and many more, drawn from 
a closer study of Sanskrit Grammar nud the works of classical authors, 
have been added to each Lesson, Throughout the book several San- 
skrit passages have been added, cither to the illustrative sentences, or 
to the sentences for exercise. The effect of this and the like additions 
has been to increase the matter by nearly one-half. Yet by a suitable 
arrangement of types, the volume of the work has not much increased, 
and that it may be within the reach of all classes of students, the 
price has been reduced to Re, 1, As. S. The rapid sale of a large edi- 
tion in less than three years shows that the book, in some measure, 
supplied a felt want; and it is hoped that the student of Sanskrit will 
find this edition more useful and a better guide to Sanskrit composi- 
tion than the first, on account of the improvements effected in it, 

Before concluding, 1 must not omit to tender my most sincere 
thanks to Dr. R. G. Bhandarkar, who was kind enough to spare time 
to go over the greater portion of the book with me, and to mako 
several important suggestions which have been mostly acted upon; 
and secondly, to Mr. Lee Warner, Acting Director of Public Instruc- 
tion who, at the recommendation of Dr. Bhandarkar, was pleascd to 
sanction the work for use in High Schools. My acknowledgements 
are also due to Dr, Morell, Professor Bain, and Mr. McMordie, whose 
works have been useful to me in writing Part IV.; und lastly to Mr. 
M. Sheshagiri Prabhu of the Madras Presidency, who was the first to 
suggest the addition of Analysis and Synthesis of sentences. 


Poona, 24th December 1885. V. 8. APTE. 


ix 
Preface to the Third Edition. 


For this edition ‘the book has been carefully revised throughout, 
and some sentences have been added, particularly in the illustration 
of rules, As the work is now used as a text-book in severa] High 
Schools, even in the other Presidencies, no material changes in ite 
plan and scope have been effected. It gives me great pleasure to find 
that the several important changes made in the second edition have 
met with general approval, and that the book affords help, however 
slight, to the student in writing Sanskrit correctly and mastering 
some of the intricacies of its idiom.

INTRODUCTORY. 


‘Syntax’ in English deals with the mode of arranging words in 
sentences and lays down rules for the proper and correct arrange- 
ment of words. In Sanskrit and other languages that are rich in 
inflexions, Syntaz has not this definite scope. The grammatical 
inflexion itself shows the relation of one word to another, and no harm 
or inaccuracy occurs, if the student does not observe the usual order of 
words in a sentence. Take, for example, the English sentence, “Rima 
saw Govinda.” If the order of the words ‘Raima’ and ‘Govinda’ be 
changed, there will be a very great difference in the meaning; it will, 
in fact, be a different sentence altogether, Take, however, the 
Sanskrit sentence for the same: रामो गोविन्दमपदयत. Here, evenif the 
order of the words be changed, no difference occurs in the meaning; 
the sentences रामो गोविन्द्मपश्यत्‌, गोविन्दं रामोा3पद्यत्‌, अपड्यद्रामो गोविन्द 
&o., all mean the same thing. The order or arrangement of words in 
Sanskrit sentences is not, therefore, a point of great importance except 
in some cases ; but this does not mean that’ perfect arbitrariness is 
allowed, and there are certain cases in which it is necessary to 
arrange words in a particular nianner. In Sanskrit Grammars, rules 
on Concord and Order are rarely given. The *Káraka-Prakarana" in 


2 THE STUDENT'S GUIDE 


the SiddAánta- Kaumudi is popularly, though not correctly, taken to 
represent Syntax in Sanskrit, but it represents only one of the parts 
of Byntax properly so called, i. e. Government. The use and meaning 
of particles and grammatical forms has also to be taken into account 
in the joining together of words into sentences. This portion of Gram- 
mar is usually considered in English Grammars under Etymology; 
and in Sanskrit Grammars, in explaining the formation of words in 
Accidence, the use of the words themselves is given; as in the Sutra 
‘oe: इहतृशानचावप्रथमासमानाधिकरणे” (Panini III. 2. 124), which states 
how to form present participles as well as when to use them. In treat- 
ing of ‘Syntax’ in Sanskrit, one has thus to look mainly to Concord 
and Government and the Use and Meaning of Grammatical Forms and 
Worde, and the Lessons in this work are accordingly arranged. 


As already remarked, the order of words is not so important a 
point in Sansrit as in English; but there are a few cases in which it 
has to.be carefully attended to. Some hints on this subject will be 
found in Part IV. 


६ 2. There are in Sanskrit, as in English and other languages, 
three persons and three genders. The use of persons is not practically 
different from what it is in English. As regards genders of nouns in 
Sanskrit, no definite rules can be laid down to distinguish one from 
another. The assignment of genders is purely arbitrary, except in 
those cases where the male and female 86508 are indicated, and where 
the distinction is natural; as, चटक ‘a male sparrow,’ चटका ‘a female 
sparrow; हैसः, हसी; अजः, अजा Kc. The arbitrariness of genders may 
well be seen from the fact that there are, in Sanskrit, three words of 
three different genders for one and the same thing; ‘ wife’ is 
represented by दार ( masc. ), भार्या (fom. ), and कलञ्ज ( neut. ); ' body ’ 
by कायः, तनुः, and grate; &c. Genders must, for the most part, be 
studied from the dictionary. 


There are three numbers, instead of two, as in English or Latin, | 
some peculiarities in the ure of which are noted below. 


TO SANSKRIT COMPOSITION. 3 


§ 3. The three numbers in Sanskrit are the singular, dual, 
and plural. The singular number denotes ‘ one’ or a single indi- 
vidual, but often represents, as in English, the whole class; as नर: 


‘a man;’ सिंहः सर्वेधापदेष «fos: ‘the lion is the strongest of 
all beasts.’ 


Note.—To representa class the singular or plural may be 
used: ‘ Bráhmanas must be respected’ may be expressed by ब्राह्मण: 
पूज्यः, ०7 ब्राह्मणाः पूज्याः. 

6 4. The dual denotes ‘two’; अश्विनो ‘the two As'vins;’ 
दंपती ‘a pait’ ( husband and wife). But words meaning a ‘couple’ 
or ‘pair,’ such as ga, द्वितय, युगल, युग, aE ६०. are always singular, 
except when several pairs are indicated; as बाहुद्दये ' & pair of arms;' 
सुकुमा रचरणयुगल ‘a pair of delicate feet.’ 

(a) The dual form sometimes denotes a ‘male’ and a ‘female’ 
belonging to the same class, the form being an instance of the 
एकशेषदइ न्द्र compound; as जगतः पितरो वन्दे पार्वतीपरमेश्वरो (8. L1) 
<I salute the parents of the’universe, Parvati and ParmeSvara (Siva).' 

$ 5. Some words having a dual sense, that occur in the plural 
form in English, ought, in Sanskrit, to betranslated by the dual 
alone; as, ‘he washed his hands and feet’ हस्तो पादौ चाक्षालठयत; ‘she 
shut hor eyes’ सा लोचने न्यमीलयत. 

S 6. The plural denotes ‘more than two,’ and may, like the 
singular, represent the whole class; शकुंताः ‘birds,’ ora ‘class of birds? 
But there are some words in Sanskrit which, though used in the 
plural, are singular in sense; as, दारा; ‘wife’; similarly sm, वर्षा, 
सिकता, अक्षता, ag, प्राण &c 

(a) Sometimes the plural is used to show respect, or to speak 


of a person with reverence; as, इति श्रीहकराचार्याः ‘so says the revered 
Samkara 


(5) In the first person the plural ‘sometimes stands for the 
singular, if the speaker is a high personage; as, वयमपि भवत्यौ सखीगतं 
किमपि प्रच्छामः (S. 1) ‘we, too, (४. e. I) ask your ladyships something 
regarding your friend’; वयमपि स्वकमण्यनियुज्यामहे (Mu.3) ‘wo, too, shall

apply ourselves to our work’. But this condition is not absolutely 
necessary; e. 9. किंत्वरण्यसदो घयमनभ्यस्तरथचर्याः (U. 5). 

S 7. Names of countries are always in Banskrit used in the 
plural, because they aretaken from the people themselves; as, अहं गतः 
कदाचित कलिंगान ( D. K. II. 7) ‘I once went to Kalinga’ (the country 
of the Kalingas ). 

N. B.—W hen the words देश, विषय &c. are used with the names of 
countries, the singular must be used; as, मगधदेदो पाटलिपुच नाम नगरं 
*there is a town called Pátaliputra in the country of the Magadhas'. 

& 8. The plural of proper nouns not infrequently denotes the 
family or race, as in English; as, रघूणामन्वयं वक्ष्ये ( R. I. 9)*I shall 
describe the family ( genealogy ) of the race of Raghu; जनकानां TINT 
च संबन्धः कस्य न प्रियः (U. 1) ‘to whom is a connexion between the 
families of Raghu and Janaka not dear £’ 


LESSON I. 


६ 9. “When two connected words aro of the same gender, num- 
ber, person, or tenso, they are said to agree with ono another or to be 
in Concord. Speaking of a man, we have to say he, of a woman 
she, of plurality of persons they; these are agreements or concords,”’ 
— Bain. 

The Concords that desorve notice in Sanskrit aro three: (1) 
Concord of Subject and Verb, (2 ) Concord of Substantive and .1djectiee, 
and (3) Concord of Relative and its Antecedent. 


Concord of Subject and Verb. 

& 10. That about which something is said or asserted is called 
the subject of a sentence, and is put in the nominative case. A verb, 
as in English, agrees with its subject in number and in person; as, 
आसीद्राजा SEI नाम (1६. 5) ‘there was a king named Sadraka;’ 
साधयामो वर्य (S. 1) ‘we go’ (take our way). 


TO SANSKRIT COMPOSITION. 5 


€ 11. The predicate, or that which is said about the subject, 
may be a finite verb, as in the above instances, or a substantive or 
an adjective with अस्‌ ‘to be," expressed or understood, In such cases 
the substantive should be used in its natural gender, being made to 
agree with the subject only in case; as, सा कुलपतेरुच्छुसितमिव (8.2) 
* ghe is, as it were, tho lifo of tho Kulapati; ककुदं वेदविदा ( Mk. 1) 
‘who is the hump (chief) of those who areconversant with the Vedas.’ 

Obs.—The concord of the adjective is given in Lesson II. 


(a) The verb, when used in such cases, always agrees with the 
subject; as तस्मात्सखा त्वमसि (U. 5) ‘thou art, therefore, a friend’. 


(P) When words like पात्र, आस्पद, स्थान, पद, प्रमाण, and भाजन, are

(a) When the subjects are of different numbers, the verb wiil 
agree with the nearest subject; as ते वाऽयं बा पारितोषिकं गृह्णातु ‘let 
them or this ( person ) take the reward.’ 

$15. When two or more nominatiyes of different persons are

LESSON II, 


Concord of Substantive and Adjective. 


§ 18. In English an adjective is used with all genders, num- 
bers and casés, in the same unaltered form; as a good man, good tables, 
M saw & good horse &c. In Sanskrit, however, all adjectives, whether 
‘participial, pronominal, or qualitative, must take the same gender, 
number, and cass as the noun which they qualify, as, गच्छन्ती नारी, का 


10 THE STUDENT'S GUIDE 


तृप्तिः, तत्खखं; शोभनानि ग्रहाणि ‘good houses’; शोभनेभ्यो गृहेभ्यः (from good 
houses’; शोभनाभ्यो बापीभ्यः ‘from good wells; हृरिं पक्ष्यन्‌ छुच्यते &c. The: 
adjective in Sanskrit must, in fact, be treated like a noun capable of 
taking cases, genders, and numbers. 

Obs. —Numeral adjectives differ from ordinary adjectives. They 
have particular rules for their use, for which see Grammar. 

8 19. When adjectives are used in Appositional or Bahuvrihi 
compounds, they are used in their original unmodified form; as, 
Jg: ‘a black deer’; रक्तनेत्रा (०1 red eyes’ (fom.); रूपवद्धार्या ‘a 
beautif-", wife’; merana: ‘a bow taken; अन्यसंक्रान्तहदयो नरः a man 
whose heart is fixed on another (lady  &e, 

(a) There area few exceptions, The sign of the feminine 
gender is retained, when the feminine is treated as an appellative, 
when an ordinai number in the feminine gender is the first member, 
or when the first member is regarded as a class-name; as, दत्ताभार्य 
पचमीभायः, SIRTHDT: &०.; also सुकेशीभायः, ardrara:. For further parti- 
culars, see SiddAdnta-Aaumudé, on Panini VI, 3. 34-41 

8 20. When participial adjectives, such as past and potential 
passive, are used as predicates, and when the subject is followed by 
an appositional noun used predicatively, the participle agrees with 
the subject (see 8 11); as, मालाविका उपायनं प्रेषिता ( M. 1) ‘Malavika 
was sent (as) a present.’ 

§ 21. When there are two or more substantives qualified by 
the same adjective, the latter is used in their combined number, As 
regards gender, when the substantives are masculine and feminine, 
the adjective will be masculine; and when they are masculino or 
feminine and neuter, the adjective will be neuter; as, पक्षपातिनावनयोरहं 
देवी च (M. 1) ‘I and the Queen are ( respectively) interested in these 
two’; तस्मिन्सत्यं धतिज्तांनं तपः शोचं दमः शमः। ध्रवाणि Wee rH लोकपालसमे

ities, purity, self-control and tranquillity, are firm in that king, 
pre-eminent among men and resembling the guardians of the worlds.’

§ 22. But an adjective in Sanskrit often agrees with the sub- 
stantive nearest to it; as, यस्य वीर्यण कृतिनो ad च भुवनानि च (U. 1) ‘by 
whose valour we are rendered happy, as also the three worlds’ 
( भुबनाने ऊतीनि ); कामश्च जंभितरणो नवयोबन च (Mal. 1 ) ‘Love has 
displayed its power, as also the blooming youth.’ Here we must 
follow what is called the लिंगविपरिणाम process; that is, the gender 
must be understood again to suit the next substantive. 


Concord of Relative and its Antecedent. 

§ 23. The concord of the relative pronoun and its antecedent 
has no special peculiarities in Sanskrit. Tho relative pronoun agrees 
with its antecedent in gender, number and person, the case of the re- 
lative being determined by its relation to its own clause. Like other 
pronouns in Sanskrit, it may stand by itself, or may be used adjec- 
tively. The relative pronoun generally precedes the noun to which 
it relates in the relativo clause; or the relative may stand alone, the 
antecedent noun being used with the demonstrative pronoun; while 
sometimes the antecedent noun is not expressed at all, अंतया सृग्यते स 
स्थाणुर्वा निःभेयसायारतु (V. 1) ‘may that Sthànu, who is inwardly 
sought, contribute to your supreme happiness;' ब्रुद्धियस्य बलं तस्य 
(P. I. 9) ‘he who has intellect has strength ' ( knowledge is power ); 
धिगस्मान्‌ सर्वान्ये एकाकिना बहुना सह युध्यामहे (fe upon us all, who are 
fighting with a single-handed boy.’ 

६ 24. When the relative has for its predicate a substantive 
differing in gender from the antecedent, tho relative generally agrees 
“tith the predicate; as, stat हि यत सा प्रछतिजेलस्य (R. V. 54) ‘for what 
ig coolness is the natural property of water;’ so मातुस्तु योतकं यत्‌ स्यात 
कुमारीभाग एब सः ( Manu. IX. 131 ).

is represented by ‘that’ in English, it is always used in the neuter

my eyes, came within the range of my sight, is tho only great

In such cases in the principal sentences, the gender of the de- 
monstrative is the same as that of the antecedent noun ( महोत्सव: ) 
and not neuter because यत is neuter.

PART Il. 


GOVERNMENT. 


LESSON HI. 
The Aceusative Case. 
६ 26. We now come to Government, the second general princi- 
ple regulating the grammatical union of words in sentences. ‘Goyern- 
ment! is the power which & word has to regulate the case of a noun or 


pronoun, The Lessons in this Part are intended to explain and 
illustrate this power. 


§ 27. 'Káraka'is the name given to the relation subsisting 
. hetween a noun and a verb in a sentence. Thus any relation subsist- 
ing bet.veen words not connected with the verb will not be called a 
Karaka. There aro six Kárakas in Sanskrit: कर्ता, कम, करण, संप्रदान, 
अपादान and अधिकरण. Theso relations belong to the first seven cases 
except the Genitive, which is not regarded as a Karaka case. कता is 
principally the senso of the Instrumental, and means ‘agent.’ The 
nominative in sanskrit, as in other languages, is simply the naming 
case, that which is concerned in अभिधान ‘predication’. According to 
Panini IT. 3.46 (प्रातिपदिका थलिंगपरिमाणवचनमात्रे प्रथमा), the nominative 
is used to denote the crude form or base of a word, gender, measure, 
and number only; as, नीचैः, कृष्णः, श्रीः, ज्ञानं, तटः-टी-टं, द्रोणो ब्रीहिः, एकः, 
at, aga: ५८०. 

Note.—Several indeclinable words govern nouns in one or an. 
other of the Káraka cases, and such cases are then called 'उपपदावेभाक्ति.' 
i. e. cases governed by indeclinables &c., as distinguished from कारक- 


विभ्रक्ति, cases governed by verbs; as, नमो नृसिंहाय, मामन्तरा, ग्रामादुत्तर Ke. 
The latter predominate over the former, where both are possible 


(उपपद्धिभक्तेः कारकविभक्तिबेलीयसी ). 


16 THE STUDENT'S GUIDE 


§ 28. The person or thing, upon whom or which the effect of 
an action takes place, is called the object of that action, An objectis - 
put in the Accusative case, except in the passive voice; as, स हरिमपञ्यत्‌ 
the saw Hari; ओदनं बुभक्षविषं भुंक्ते (5. K.) ‘wishing to eat food he 
eats poison.’ Hore हरि and (qq are objects of the. verbs amaaa and 
भुक्ते. But in हरि: सेव्यते the passive form सेव्यते expresses the relation 
of object and verb which exists between हरि and सेव, and therefore 
हरि is not required to be in the Accusative case; but in हरिं सेवते, 
there being no passivo termination, the noun हरि is put in the Accu- 
sative case. 

& 29. Verb signifying ‘to name,’ ‘to choose,’ ‘to make,’ ‘to ap- 
point,’ to call,’ ‘to know, ‘to consider,’ &c, and the like, govern a 
factitive or indirect object, besides a direct; as, त्वामामनान्ति qzía (Ku, 
II. 13 ) ‘ they consider theo to be Prakriti) mÑ गणिकामवरोधम- 
करोत ( Dk. IT. 6 ) “ made a certain courtesan his wife; जानामि aT 
प्रकतिपुरुषं (Me. ¢) * I know thee (to bo) the chief person (minister)’,

as गतोऽहं कामदेवायतनं ( Màl. 1 ) ‘I had gone to the temple of Oupid;’ 
अहमपि महीमटन्‌ ( Dk. 11. 2) ‘1 also roaming over tho earth;! यसुना- 
कच्छमवतीर्ण: ( P. I. 1) went down tothe bank of the Jummns; so 
विचचार दावं ( R. II. 8). Bnt this idea of motion is expressed in a 
variety of idiomatic expressions, where the motion is not actual but 
merely conceived; as, qi विषादमगच्छत (P. I. 1)‘was greatly dejected;’ 
अश्वत्थामा किं न यातः स्मृति त (Vo. 3) ‘was not Asvattháman thought of 
by you? पश्चादुमाख्यां nadt जगाम (Ku. I. 26)*the fair-faced lady 
afterwards went by (acquired ) the name Umi; so नरपतिहितकता 
gerat याति लोके (P. i. 2); न तृप्तिमाययों ( R. 111. 3).

LESSON IV. 
Verbs governing two Accusatives. 

§ 38. There are some verbs in Sanskrit which take what is 
called an akathtta (अकथित) object, in addition to their usual direct 
one, As its name indicates, it is that object which is not otherwise 
kathita (कचिल) or mentioned by way of any of the other case rela- 
tions, such as अपादान, आधिकरण &c., and is, therefore, op&onal. If 
the noun capable of taking this akathsia object be not intended for 
any other case, it is put in the Accusative case with such verbs, as, 
wd दोग्धि पयः ‘he milks the cow (her milk); व्रजमवरुणद्धि गां ‘he 
confines the cow to the fold.’ Here wa and ब्रज are okathita or 
optional objects. Ifthe speaker do not intend to have this object, 
the words will be put in their natural cases; as, Wer: (ablative) 
पयो दोग्धि, wat (locative) अवरुणद्धि गां. 

8 39. The roots that are capable of governing two accusatives 
are mentioned in the following Ka'rikd :— 

दुह्या चप चदण्ड्रुधिप्राच्छिचिब्रशासाजिमन्थमषाम्‌ | 


कमयक स्यादकथितं तथा स्यान्नीहृकुष्वहाम्‌ ॥ 

In the case of the roots qq ‘to milk,’ याच्‌ ‘to beg,’ qax ‘to cook,’ 
qug ‘to punish,’ रुध्‌ ‘to obstruct,’ or ‘to confine,’ req ‘to ask,’ fa 
‘to collect,’ gr ‘to tell,’ शास्‌ ‘to instruct,’ जि ‘to win’ (asa prize of 
wager), मन्थ्‌ ‘to churn,’ gw ‘to steal,’ and also in the case of नी, हु, 
ww, and ax, all meaning ‘to take’ or ‘carry,’ and others having the 
aame signification, the noun which, besides the direct object, is 
affected by the verb, is put in the Accusative case; as, गां दोग्धि पयः 
(B, K.) ‘he milks the cow,’ बलि याचते veut (ibid.) ‘he begs the Earth 
of Bali’; similarly, तण्डुलानोदन पचाति, गगाच शतं दण्डयति, व्रज़॒मवरुणद्धि 
गां, माणवकं dai प्रच्छाति, रक्षमवचिनोति फलानि, माणवर्क धर्म हृते-शास्ति, 
शर्त जयति देवदत्तं, क्षीरनिधि gut मथ्नाति, देवदत्त शतं gent, ग्राममजां

नयाति-हराति-कषाति-वहाति वा, are examples of the other roots in order. 
माणवकं wu भाषते-वक्ति वा, बलिं agai भिक्षते, तां त्वां संवरणस्यार्थ वरयामि

roots given in the Kárik$&. 

Obs. The roots चि, खुष. पत्र, मन्थ, रुध, जि, HT, g, and even बह, 
are of very rare occurrence, as governing two accusatives, in 
classical literature, though given in the above list. 

840. The roots mentioned above and others, having the same 
sense, thus take two objects. One of them is principal, and the other 
secondary. In the case of the first twelve roots from gg to gq, 
the nouns पयः, वसुधां, फलानि, gut &c, are principal objects, and गां, 
बलिं, दक्ष, क्षीरानाधि &c , are secondary objects; for they can, according 
to the speaker's volition, be put in other cases. And in the case 
of the last four roots, अजा is the principal object, and ग्रामं, the 
secondary, Thus, that which is necessarily put in the Accusative 
ease in order to complete the idea of the verb, is the principal object, 
and that which may be put in the Accusative case, depending upon 
the speaker's will, is called the secondary object. 

§ 41. *In the passive construction of roots governing two 
acousatives, the secondary object in the case of the first twelve roots, 
and the principal object iu the case of the last four, is put in the 
nominativo case, the other object remaining the same as in the 
active construction : e. g.,

LESSON V. 
The Causal. 

8 42. “The causal of a root conveys the notion that a person 
or thing causes or makes another person or thing to perform. the 
action or to undergo the state denoted by the root” ( Dr. Kielhorn’s 
Grammar § 424 ); e. g., गम्‌ (to go’, गच्छाति ‘he goes’, गमयति ‘he causes 

"te go’; अड्‌ ‘to eat’, अश्षाति ‘he eats’, आशयाति ‘he causes ts eat.’ 
§ 43. That which is the subject of a verb in its primitive sense 


is put in the Instrumental case in the causal, the object remaining 
unchanged; e. g.,

i food.

Ráma abandons (his) wife, ' (He) makes Ráma adandon 


| œ (his) wife,

Note.—Patafijali, in his Mahàbháshya, adds this explanation on 
the meaning of the word डाब्दकर्म in the Sutra afagte &८. शब्दकर्म 
may mean either gA येषां क्रिया or झाब्दो येषां कर्म. When we take the 
former interpretation, the roots aqfa (हवे), क्रन्दति (क्रन्द) and शब्दायते 
( denom. of zraz ) have to be excluded from the rule; as, gata देवदत्तः,

And the roots श्रु, ज्ञा with वि, and लभ with gq, must be included in 
the rule; as डाणोति-विजानाति-उपलभत-देवदृत्तः, भावयाते-विज्ञापयाति-उपल- 
म्भयति-देवदत्तम. When we adopt the second interpretation, the roots 
जल्प्‌, भाष with आ, and gy with वि, must be included in the rule; 
जल्पति-आभाषते-विलपति-देवदृत्त, जल्पयति-आभाषयति-बिलापयति-देव दृत्तं.

§ 46. By ‘intransitive roots’ mentioned in § 44 are meant such 
roots as are not, by their nature, capable of governing an object 
other than that of ‘time’, ‘place’ &c., and not those roots which, though 
transitive, may sometimes be used intransitively according to the 
speaker’s volition, or when their meaning is quite evident; as किंकरः 
पचाति. Here पचाति, though transitive, is used without an object, 
because it can be easily understood; hence किंकरेण पाचयाति and not 
किंकरं; but मासमासयति देवदत्त.

LESSON VI, 
The Instrumental Case. 

& 50. *The Instrumental case in Sanskrit has two principal 
senses: it either denotes the agent of an action, or the instrument or 
means by which the action is done ; as, ततो देव्या किमाभिहितम्‌ (Ve. 1) 
‘then what was said by the Queen °’; संचुणयामि गद्या न सुयोधनोरू 
(Ve. 1 ) ‘shall I not reduce to powder the thighs of Suyodhana with 
my club?’ तामेब दिव्ययोषितं चक्षुषा पुनानरूपयामास (K. 131) ‘again 
looked at that same celestial woman with his eye.’ 

§ 51. The instrumentality, which this case denotes, may be 
expressed by various relations:— 

" («) The manner of doing an action, or an attribute which 
characterizes a noun ; आत्मानुरूपां विधिनोपयेमे (Ku. 1. 18) ‘he married 
(her) worthy of himself according to the rite’; प्रकृत्या दर्शानीयः (Mbh.) 
‘lovely by nature’; माठरोऽस्मि गोत्रेण ( 14.) ‘I am Màthara by my 
family-name'; विषमेण धावति (10id.) ‘walks unevenly’; similarly द्विद्रोणेन 
धान्यं क्रीणाति, सहस्रेण पशून्‌ क्रीणाति, शतेन शतेन पाययाते घत्सान्‌, &c. 

(b) The price at which a thing is bought ; as, कियता मूल्येन क्रीतं 
पुस्तकं ‘at what price was the book bought ?' 

(c) “With verbs indicating motion, the conveyance becomes the 


instrument ; as, आत्मनः qd बिमानेन विगाहमानः ( R. XIII. 1 ) * passing 
through his own abode (the sky) in a balloon.’

*कतकरणयोस्तर्तीया । ( 11. 3. 18 )

(d) With verbs of carrying or placing, that on which a thing is 
carried or placed is put in the Instrumental; as, स श्वानं स्कन्धेनोवाह 
( H. 4 ‘he bore the dog on his shoulder’; भतुराज्ञां मूर्ध्ना आदाय 
( Ku. IIL. 22 ) * placing his master’s order on his head.’ 

(e) With words of * swearing,’ that, in whose name the oath is 
taken, becomes the instrument; as, जीवितेनेव शपामि ते ( K. 293) ʻI 
swear to thee by my very life.’ 

(f) The direction or route followed to go to a particular place 
also becomes the instrument ; as कतमेन दिग्भागेन गतः स जाल्मः ( V.1) 
‘in what direction did the rogue go ?' 

§ 53. With verbs implying ‘excelling’ and * resembling ? the 
Instrumental is used with the qualities in which the excellence 
consists, or the points or particulars referred to in the resemblance; 
a3, पूर्वान्महाभाग तसा 5तिहाषे (R. V. 14) *O you fortunate one, you excel 
your ancestors in that ( devotion )’; स्वरेण रामभद्रमनुहराते ( ए. 4 ) 
‘resembles Rama in his voice.’ 

Obs.—Sometimes the Locative is used in the same sense; as, 
धनदेन समस्त्यागे सत्ये धम इवापरः ( Ram. I. I. 19 ) ‘equal to Kubera in 
(point of) charity ( munificence ), and like another Dharma (a second 
Dharma ) in truthfulness’. 

(a) Words implying ‘separation from’, are usually construed 
with the Instrumental; as, अयमेकपदे तया वियोग उपनतः ( V. 4) ‘here 
is this separation from her suddenly fallen to my lot’; so मा भूदेवं 
क्षणमपि च ते विद्युता विप्रयोगः ( Me. 118), 

(b) Words expressing ‘likeness’ or ‘equality’ are also used with 
this case ; as, uaaa समस्त्य"गे ‘equal to Kubera in munificence ; अस्य 
सुखं सीताया मुखचन्द्रेण संवदात ( U. 4) ‘lis face corresponds to (is just 
similar to) the moonlike face of Sit See under Genitive also. 

§ 53. *The Instrumental is used with words expressive of. 
time or place when the accomplishment of the desired object is meant `

to be expressed; as ह्वाद्शवर्षेव्याकरणं श्रूयत (P. 1.) ‘grammar is learnt in

§ 54. *When a noun denotes the cause or motive of a thing or 
action, as distinguished from the means or instrument, it is put in 
the Instrumental case; as, गुरो भक्त्या प्रीताऽस्मि ते ( R. 11. 63 ) ५ am 
pleased with thee for thy devotion to thy preceptor’; आतिदवीयस्तया

(a) The object 0% purpose also is put in the Instrumental case; 
as अध्ययनेन वसति (8. K. ) dwells with the view ( for the purpose ) 
of studying.’ 

Obs.—The Instrumental, which is used with verbs meaning ‘to be 
satisfied,’ ‘to rejoice at,’ ‘to be astonished at,’ ‘to be ashamed of,’ 
is accounted for by this rule; as, कापुरुषः स्वल्पकेनापि तुष्पाति (P.I. 1 ) 
'*& low person is pleased even with little’; उभयोर्न तथा लोकः प्राषाण्येन 
विसिष्मिये ( R. XV. 68 ) ‘tho people did not so much wonder at tke 


proficiency of both of them’; अनेन प्रागल्भ्येन war ( K. 103 ) ‘I am 
ashamed of this boldness.’ 


§ 55 f Attributives, which show some defect of the body, 
govern the (word expressive of the) defectivelimb in the Instrumental 
०886; 83 अक्ष्णा काण:( S.K. ) ‘blindofone eye’: so पादेन गवः, कणन 
ब्धिरः, etc. 

६ 56. t Anattribute, which indicates the existence of a parti- 
cular state or condition, is putin the Instrumental case to express 
this relation; as जटाभिस्तापसः ( S. K.) ‘he is an ascetic by ( the fact 
of his having ) matted hair.’ 

§ 57. अलं and aw, meaning ‘enough,’ govern this case; as अल- 
मतिविस्तरेण ( Vo. | ) ‘enough of prolixity’; कृतमश्वेन (U 4) ‘away 
with the horse’; तस्मात्कतं चरणपाताबिड म्बनाभिः ( P. IV. 1).

(a) In this sense अलं is often used with a gerund; as, अलमन्यथा

In such cases it has a prohibitive force.

mansion,’

or need, and the root x; with कि when used in this sense, govern the 
Instrumental of that which is used or needed, and the Genitive of the 
user; as, देवपादानां सेवकेन प्रयोजनं (H. 1) ‘your Majesty's feet have no 
need of servants’; तृणेन काये भवतीश्वराणां (P. I. 1) ofrich persons 
(even) some purpose is served by a blade of grass’; के तया क्रियते west 
(P. I.) ‘what is to be done with that cow ?’; कि तया दृष्टया (S. 2) ‘what 
is the good of seeing her?; अप्राज्ञेन सानुरागेण भूत्येन को gor: (Mu. 1) 
‘what is the use of a devoted but foolish servant ?’

LESSON VII. 
The Dative Case. 

8 60. The person to whom something is given is called संप्रदान. 
A noun denoting संप्रदान is putin the Dative caso: as किं वस्तु विद्वन्‌ 
गुरवे qmd (R. V. 18) ʻO learned Sir, what is to be given to the 
preceptor?' "The person or thing with reference to whom or having 
in view which an action is done is also संप्रदान; 83 युद्धाय संनद्याते 
( Mbh. ) ‘he prepares for battle; तां नन्दनाय प्रार्थयते (४६), 1) ‘he 
demands her for Nandana.’ 

(a) *In the caso of the root aq ‘to sacrifice’ or ‘to give as in 
a sacrifice,’ the person to whom the sacrifice is offered is put in 
the Accusative, and the thing or means by which it is made is put 
in the Instrumental; as, पशुना रुद्रे यजते (S. K.) ‘he sacrifices a bull 
to Rudra,’ 

8 61. | In the case of the root रूच ‘to like,’ and others having 
the same signification, the person or thing that is pleased or satisfied 
is put in the Dative cage; as, यत्प्रभाविष्णवे रोचते (S. 2 ) ‘what pleases 
your Lordship’; यज्ञदत्ताय स्वद्ते5पूपः (KASiká) 'Yajüadatta likes 
Aptpa.’ 

862. + The person to whom something is due ( the creditor ) 
in the case of the root zw (10 ol.) ‘to owe,’ and the thing desired in 
the cage of eye, are put in tho Dative case ; as दृक्षसचने d धारयसि मे 
(S'. 1) ‘thou owest me two sprinklings of trees’; परिक्षीणो यवानां 
प्रसृतये gafa (Bh, III 45) ‘an impoverished person desires a hand- 
ful of barley-corn.’ 

Obs.—Derivatives from gg are sometimes construed with the 
Dative; as, भोगेभ्यः स्पृहयालव: (Bh. III, 64) ‘desirous of enjoyments’; 
कथमन्ये करिष्यन्ति पुत्रेभ्यः पत्रिणः स्पृहां (Ve. 3); but generally with the 
Locative; wggradt वस्तुषु केषु मागधी (R. IIT, 5).

§ 65. ł The purpose for which an action is done, or that for 
making which another thing exists or is used (as a thing made for 
a certain purpose) is put in the Dative case; as काव्यं यशस (Kav. 1) 
‘a poem (is composed) for fame’; सूपाय दारू ( Mbh ) ‘a piece of wood 
for (making a sacrificial) post’; कुंडलाय हिरण्यं (४274.) «gold is (used) 
for Kundala (ornament); अवहननाय उलूखलं (ibid. ) ‘a mortar for 
pounding down.’ 

(a) § When the sense of an infinitive of purpose is supprossed 
in a sentence, the object of this infinitive is put in the Dative case; 
as, फलेन्यो याते =फलान्याहतु याति ‘ he goes for fruits,’ + e ‘to bring 
fruits’; बनाय गां gHi—44 गन्तुं गां सुमोच ‘he let loose the cow 
for (i. e. to go to) the forest.’ Here फल and qq, tho objects of the 
infinitives MET and गन्तुं, are put in the Dative. 

(b) + The Dative of an abstract novn is 01001 used to express 
the sense of the infinitive of purpose from the root; as, यागाय arfa= 
qa याति ‘he goes to offer a sacrifice’; so समिदाहरणाय प्रस्थिता वयं (5. 1); 
यतिष्ये वः सखीप्रत्यानयनाय (V. 1).

LESSON VIII. 
The Ablative Case. 

§ 72. The chief sense of the Ablative case is अपादान, That 
from wlich separation, whether actual or conceived, takes place is 
called अपादान and is put in the. Ablative case; as, ग्रामादायाति ‘ he 
comes from a village’; i. e., that from which the separation takes 
place is ग्राम. It has thus the sense of * from ° in English. 

९१3. *Anouninthe Ablative case frequently denotes the 
cause of an action or phenomenon, and has the sense of ‘on account 
of’, ‘for’, ‘hy reason of &c.; as साहू fagamraar ( U. 1) not living 
apart (whose resort was not difterent) on account ofaffection " A noun, 
uot of the fominine gender, denoting the ७1139 of an action. 13 put 
ly the Instrumental or Ablative ; as, araga stearga वा qp: ( 5. K. ) 

he was caught by reson of his dalness; बुद्धचा Qm: CS. K.)

( thy ) preceptor and compassion on me.'

(a) The Ablative often serves the purpose of a whole causati ve

& 74. Words in the comparative degree, or such as have the 
sense of comparison, are used with the Ablative of that with refer- 
ence to which the comparison is made; as सत्यादप्यनृ्त श्रेयः ( Ve. 3) 
‘falsehood is better even than tru'h'; मोहादभूत्कष्टतरः प्रबोधः ( R. XIV.

भधसहस्रेभ्यः सत्यमेवातिरिच्यते ( H. 4) ‘truth itself is superior to & 
thousand horse-sacrifices’; श्राद्धस्य पूर्वाह्णादपराह्णो विशिष्यत ( Manu. III. 
218 ) ‘for a Sráddha ceremony afternoon is preferred to forenoon’.

प्रासादात्पेक्षते ( 5. £. ) प्रासादमारुहय प्रेक्षते ‘sees from a palace’; so 
ag qna ( 8. £. ) = श्वशुरं atur ree.

gafa प्रेक्षते ‘sees from a seat.’

LESSON IX. 
The Locative Case. 

§ 87. The place, sn or on which an action is represented as 
taking place, is called अधिकरण, and is put in the Locative case; as, 
स्थाल्यामोदनं पचति ‘cooks food in a cooking-utensil’; आसने उपविशाति 
* gits on a seat.’ 

(a) The Locative is used to denote the time when an action 
takes place; as, आपषाटस्य प्रथमदिवसे ( Me. 2) ‘on the first day of 
A'shádha'; ४० शेशवेऽभ्यस्तविद्यानां योबने विषयेषिणाम ( R. 1. 8 ). 

8 88. The Locative has very often the sense of ‘towards,’ 
` about’, ‘as to’ &o.; as, मयि मा भ्रकरुणा ( Mal. 9) ‘be not ruthless 
towards me’; विषयेषु विनाशधर्मंस निःस्प्रहो$भवत ( R. VIII. 10 )* became 
free from desire for perishable objects. ’ 

§ 89. *With adjectives in the superlative degree, and in those 
cases where a distinction is made, as of an individual from his 
whole class, ( generally expressed by ‘of’, or ‘among’ in English ), 
the nouns, with respect to which such pre-eminence or distinction is 
shown, are put in the Genitive or Locative; as, गवां गोषु WT रूष्णा 
बहुक्षीरा (8. K. ) ‘among cows the black one gives much milk’; so 
` नृणां नृषु वा दिजः wg: ( ibid. )

§ 95. When a word indicating ‘cause’ is used, ‘the effect’ is 
often put in the Locative: as, दैवमेव हि ami cxt क्षये कारण (Bb. 11.४4)

of men’. 

& 98. The root युज and its derivatives govern the Locative of 
the thing in the sense of ‘io’ in English; as, असाधुदर्शी तत्रभवान 


काइयपो य इमामाशमधर्म नियुङ्क्ते ( 5. 1) ‘the revered Kasyapa is not 
prudent, as he appoints her to the duties of the hermitage. 


(a) With words expressive of ‘fitness’, ‘suitableness’ &e., the 
nouns regarding which the fitness is expressed are put in the Doca- 
tive case: 85, युक्तरूपामिदं त्वयि ( S. 2) ‘this is fit for thee’; Xerar 
sued तस्मिन्युज्यते (H. 5) ‘the sovereignty of even the three worlds 
suits him’: अथवबोपपन्नमेतदृषिकल्पे;स्मिन्राजानि (9. 2) ८०7 this is quite 
proper for this sage-like king’; ते aur: परस्मिन्‌ ब्रह्मण्युपपद्यन्ते (5. B.190) 
‘those attributes suit the Supreme Brahma. * 

Obs. —'The Genitive is not infrequently used in the same sense: 
as, ZAA विशषणं वायो: ( V. 2 ) ‘this epithet suits the wind.’ 

६ 97. The Locative, strictly speaking, implies place, but in 
several cascs it is used with the object or recipient to which anything 
is entrusted or imparte: as, छशुकनासनाम्नि afato राज्यनारमाराप्य 
यावनसखमनुबभ्व ( K. 57 ) ‘having entrusted the responsibilities of

LESSON X, 


The Genitive Case. 





§ 101. Tho Genitive case, as observed in Lesson III., is not a 
Karaka ease, and, strictly speaking, expresses the relation of one 
noun to another in a sentence. In the rules given in this Lesson 
the Genitive has one principal sense, viz. संबन्ध, and even in those 
cases where verbs are used with the Genitive, it is to be considered 
as having tho sense of relation only. But in several instances this 
case is used by loose, and not infrequently even classical, authors, 
to express relations pertaining to other cases; a3, त च व्यसजद्धरतस्य 
( U. 4 ) ‘sont it to Bharata’ ( भरतस्य being put for भरताय ); जयसना- 
` यास्तावत्संवय गच्छ ( . 4) for जयसेनाये &०.; स्रीणां विश्वासो नेव Eev: 
(H. 1) for स्त्री &c. Such constructions should bo considered as 
deviations from the usual practice, aud should not be imitated. 


68 THE STUDENT'S GUIDE 


€ 102. Generally speaking, the Genitive case denotes the 
dependence of a substantive or pronoun upon another word, which 
is usually a substantive or adjective, but sometimes a verb. 

(a) Itis thus used to represent ‘of’ in English; but in several 
cases compounds are used instead of the Genitive; as, ददारथस्य पत्रः 
or ददारथपुत्रः ‘the son of Dasaratha. ’ 

Obs. Mark that all the relations expressed by ‘of’ in English 
cannot be expressed by the Genitive in Sanskrit; e. y. the adjective 
meaning and the apposition meaning; as, ‘a pot of gold’ is generally 
translated by a compound, हेमपात्रं, or by a derivative, हेमं पात्रं, but not 
by gw: पात्रं; ^a pot of ८1४५,  सुद्धाण्ड or सून्मयं भाण्डं; ‘a pearl of great 
price,’ महाघ मुक्ताफलं: ‘aman of strength,’ सबलो नरः, not रलस्य नरः. 
Similarly, ‘in the month of Vais&kha,' वेशाखे मासे or वेशाखमासे, but 
not वेशाखस्य मासे: ‘the town of Bombay,’ मुंबापुरी or gar नाम पुरी. 

So in Latin: ‘a man of talent’ is ‘homo ingeniosus’ ( धीमान्नरः ), 
not ‘homo ingenii’; but ‘a man of great talent’ is, unlike Sanskrit, 
‘homo magni ingenii. 

६103. The Genitive denotes the possessor, or the person or 
thing whereto anything belongs, that which belongs, or is possessed, 
being put in the Nominative case; as, यस्य नास्ति स्वयं प्रज्ञा P. 1. ) ‘he 
who himself possesses or has no talent’; इसे नो wet: ( Mk. I) * this 
. is our house’, ( to err belongs to men’ स्खलनं मनुष्याणां घर्मः. 

Obs. This sense is often expressed by derivatives; as, der 
रिक्थं ‘property belonging to ancestors’; so अस्मदायं ग्रहं &c. 

§ 104. The Genitive is used with substantives, and words used 
substantively, to denote the whole of whicha part is taken, and it is 
then called ‘partitive Genitive’; as, जलस्य बिंदु: ‘a drop of water’; अयुतं 
शरदां ययौ ( R. X, 1 ) ‘ten thousand years passed’; so गवां शतसहस्राणि, 
< thousands of cows, ’ 

So in Latin: ‘Mille hominum valentium’, ‘a thousand of strong 
mon.” 


TO SANSKRIT COMPOSITION. 69 


(«) The Partitive Genitive is also found used with ordinals 
and pronouns or adjectives implying a number; as, त्वमेव कल्याणि 
तयोस्तृतीया ( R. Vl. 89 ) «blessed girl, you alone are their third’; 
ग्रह्मतामनयोरन्यतरा ( M. 5) ‘let one of the two be accepted’; तासाम- 
न्यतमा (Mâl. 1) ‘one of those ( girls ).’ 


Compare Latin: ‘primi juvenum, and ‘consulum alter 


(5) The Partitive Genitive is likewise used with superlatives 
and words having the force of superlatives ; as द्विजानां ब्राह्मणः श्रेष्ठ:ः 
धोरेयः साहसिकानामग्रणीरविंदग्धानां (K. 5 ) ‘the foremost among the 
bold, and among the clever. ’ 


Obs. This use of the Genitive is already considered in § 89. 


So in Latin: *'Mazimi principum’ ‘greatest of princes’; ‘ oratorum 


praestantissimi ' ‘the most eminent of orators.’ 


(c) Sometimes मथ्य is used with the Genitive in the sense of 
‘of’ or ‘among’; as, एतेषां मध्ये केचिदरेः कोषदण्डाभ्यामार्थिनः ( Mu. 5 ) ‘of 
these some are desirous of the treasure and army of the enemy.’ 

8 105. When a certain period is represented to have elapsed 
after the occurrence of an action, the word expressing the occurrence 
is put in the Genitive case; as अद्य दडामो मासस्तातस्योपरतस्य ( Mu. 6) 
‘itis to-day ten months since the death of my father’; कतिपये 


संवत्सरास्तस्य तपस्तप्यमानस्य ( U. 4 ) ‘several years have elapsed since 
he began to practise penance.’ 


§106. Words having the sense of ‘dear to’ or the reverse 


are used with this case; as qaaa प्रिया सीता रामस्यासीत (U. 6) ` Sitá 
was by hor very nature dear to Rama’; कायः कस्य न apa: ( P. I.) ‘to 
whom is the body not dear ? 


~- (a) This ease also occurs in the construction of words signify- 


ing ‘difference’, such as, विशेषः, अंतरं; as, एतावानेषायुष्मतः दतक्रतोश्व

much difference between this worthy person and myself as between

§ 107. *¥In the case of potential passive participles, the agent 
of the action is put in the Genitive or Instrumental case; as, नास्त 
असाध्यं नाम मनोभुवः (K. 157) indeed, there is nothing that cannot be

8108. + With words meaning ‘cause’, ‘sake’, ‘reason,’ the 
Genitive is used, as, अल्पस्य हेताबंह हातुमिच्छन्‌ (R. 11. 47 ) ‘wishing to 
give up ( lose ) much for the sake of little’; विस्मूर्त कस्य हेतोः (Mu. 1) 
‘for what reason is it forgotten ?’ 


Obs. Patafijali says that the words meaning ‘cause’, ‘reason, 
such as निमित्त, कारण, हेतु may be used in this sense in any case in 
agreement with pronouns. But this is not universally supported by 
the usage of classical authors. केन निमित्तन-कारणेन-हेठुना and कस्मान्निमि- 
त्तात-कारणात-हेतोः, are the usual constructions in this senso. We do 
not say को हेतु: बससि or क॑ gd बसास in the same sense, nor evon qu 
zaa बसासि which means ‘ for what object ( purpose ) in view do you 
dwell? किनिमित्त-प्रयोजनं-कारणं-अर्थ is, however, not uncommon, 
Patanjali’s rule must, therefore, be understood in a restricted sense. 

8109. f With nouns derived from roots by Krit afüxes, such 
a3 ति, तू, अ, अन &c., the Genitive is used is the sense of the agent 
and object of the action denoted by the nouns; in other words, the 
Genitive in Sanskrit is both subjective and objective; as, क्रियामिमां 
कालिदासस्य (४. 1) ‘this composition of Kalidasa’, भर्तुः प्रणाशात्‌ 
( R. XIV. 1 ) ‘on account of the death of their lord’; शासतराणां परिचयः

( K. 18 ) ‘the knowledge of Sástras'; आहर्ता maat ( K. 5) ‘the

LESSON XI. 
The Genitive And Locative Absolutes. 


€ 120. ‘When the participle agrees with a subject, different 
from the subject of the verb, the phrase is said to be in the Absolute 
construction.’ ( Bain). The phrase is unconnected with the genera". 
structure of the clause*in which it stands ; 48, ‘the wind, being 
favourable, the ship sct sail’ The Absolute case differs in differen, 


TO SANSKRIT COMPOSITION. 79 


languages; in English, it is the nominative; in Latin, the ablative; 
and in Sanskrit, the genitive and locative. If it be found that the 
nominative of the subordinate sentence be not a noun occurring in 
the principal sentence, or a pronoun representing such a noun, the 
Absolute construction may be used. Take the sentence: ‘Rama, after 
he had taken Lanka, returned to Ayodhyá. Here the subjects of 
both the sentences are the same, and no absolute construction can be 
used. The sentence may be translated by लंकां गृहीत्वा (or ग्रहातलंक: ) 
रामो5योघ्यां Aaga. But the sentence ‘Rima, after the monkeys had 
taken Lanka, returned to Ayodhy&’, may be translated by कपिभिग्रही- 
तायां लंकायां ( or कपिषु लंकां ग्रहीतवत्छ ) रामोऽयोध्यां ragà. 

Note.—To form these Absolutes, the subject of the participle 
must be put in the Genitive or Locative case, and the participle 
made*to agree with it in gender and number. 

§ 121. * A noun or pronoun, which expresses a thing the 
action done or suffered by which indicates the time of another action 
is put in the Locative case, i. e. the time of the first action is supposed 
to be known, and that of the second, which is unknown, is deter- 
mined with reference to it; as, कः (RA वसुमती शासति अविनयमाचराते 
( S. 6) ‘who, whilo Pauravais ruling the Earth, acts immodestly £’; 
वचस्यवसिते तस्मिन्‌ ससर्ज गिरमात्मभूः ( Ku. II. 53) ‘that speech being 
finished, the self-existent ( Brahmá ) uttered the words’. क एष माये 
स्थिते चन्द्रणप्तमभिभावतामेच्छात ( Mu. 1 ) ‘who, while I am still living, 
wishes to overcome Chandragupta 2’ 

Obs. The Locative Absolute in Sanskrit is used in the sense 
of the nominativo absolute in English. 

§ 122. When ‘contempt’ or ‘disregard’ is to be shown, the

‘sometimes ( now ) he plunged into a forest, and sometimes (now )

LESSON XIII. 
Participles. 


S141. All participles in Sanskrit, exeept the so-called 
indeclinable past participles or absolutives, are to be regarded as 
adjectives, agreeing with the nouns they quatify, in gender, number, 
“nd ease. They are called ‘participles’ from tho supposed parti- 
ctpation or sharing in the functions of the verb, the adjeetive. 
and the noun. The princip kinds of yarticiples in Sanekrit are 


94 THE 8TUDENT'S GUIDE 


these:— Present, Past, Future, Perfect, Potential Passive, and Inde 
olinable Past Participles; (for the rules of formation see Grammar) 
These participles obey the same rules for governing cases that 
may have been laid down regarding the roots from which they 
are derived, Present, Future and Perfect Participles are treated 
in this lesson. 

Present Participles. 

$142. The present participle in Sanskrit ( for rules of for- 
mation see Dr. Kielhorn's Grammar 8'498-500 ) corresponds to the 
participle in English ending in ‘ing.’ Itis used when contem- 
poraneity of action is indicated; as, इति विचारयज्नेष तुरगादवतार ( K. 
195 ) ‘while thinking in this manner, he dismountend from his 

orse'; विबाहकातुकं बिभ्रत एव तस्य TET हस्तगामिनीमकरोत ( R. VIII. 1) 
‘he made over the «rth to him while ( yet ) wearing the marriage- 
string; व्रजंश्च समथयामास ( K. 141 ) ‘and going, he thought.’ 

The sense of ‘while’, ‘whilst’ is thus inherent in this participle 
which serves to express an idea expressed in English by a whole 
sentence, 

Obs. (a) The Sanskrit participle must nevor be confounded 
with the participial substantive or gerund in English which also 
ends in tng. 

(5) When no contemporaneity of actionis indicated, this 
participle cannot bo used; 88, ‘ascending the mountain, they rested 
for some time; पर्वतमारुद्य ते कंचित्‌ काले व्यश्राम्यन्‌ and not पर्वतमारोहन्त: 
&c., unless the sentence implies that both actions are performed at 
the same time. 

(c) The present participle is not used in the nominative case 
a8 a predicative adjective. We do not say स qw ‘he is doing 
though we say काय कुवन्‌ क्रोडात

note ‘disposition’ or ‘habit’, ‘some standard of ave,’ and ‘ability’ or |

Future Participles. 

§ 148. The future participle which ends in स्यत्‌ (or ष्यत्‌) (pass. 
मान) denotes that a person or thing is going, or is about, to do the 
action, or to undergo the state, expressed by the root; as, करिष्यन्‌ 
‘going to do’ or ‘about to do';mrew4 ‘going to loosen’; करिष्यमाण 
‘being about to be done.’ 

(a) Besides showing simple futurity, is denotes intention or pur- 
pose; as, बन्यान्विनेष्पाचिव दष्टसच्वान्स दावं विचचार (R. IL 8) he ranged 
over the forest asif wishing to tame the wild beasts; करिष्यमाणः 
सहारं झारासनं (R. LIL. 52) ‘intending to fit an arrow to his bow,’ 
This participle thus corresponds to the English prospective form. 

Vote. Such sentenees as ‘before taking his departuro, he drank 
a little water’ are translated by means of the future participle made 
to qualify the subject, ४३ प्रयाणं करिष्यन्‌ स किचिजलं qui. ‘Before’ 
has here the seuse of ‘going’ or ‘about to take’ Ke 

Perfect Participles.

done; as भ्रयांसि सरवाण्यारिजग्मुषस्त (R ४. 34) ‘of thee who hast 
obtained all good things ( blessings )'; निषेद्षीमासनबन्धधीर: ( R. 
II. ७ ) ‘firmly maintaining his seat when sho had sat down.’

LESSON XIV. 
Past Participles. 

$150. There sre two kinds of past participles; one is passiee: 
formed by the addition of a or न to the root, and the other aetire, 
formed by adding qq to the passive participle; as, तेनेदसक्त ‘this war 
said by him’; स हदसक्तवान ‘he said this’. They are both used in the 
sense of the past tense. In later Sanskrit it became more usual to use 
participles than verbs. We generally find मया तत्कृतं ०7 भह तत्कतवाम 
instead of अहे तदकरवम, and many purposes of the predicate are 
served by this participle.

and they, as well as the past participles of transitive verbs used ir- 
‘transitively, are often used tmpersonal/y with an instrumental con-

(T. 7) ‘victorious (all-powerful) is the affection for children.’

Potential Passive Participies. 

§ 155. There aro three ways in which potential passive parti- 
ciples are formed in Sanskrit:—( 1 ) by तव्य, (2) by अनीय and 
( 9 ) by a ( for the rules of formation vide Dr, Kielhorn’s Grammar 
६९ 529-538 ); as कतव्य, करणीय, and कार्य, They perform a very useful 
function inthe economy of the Sanskrit tongue, and enable it to 
express in one word an idea which would require many words i 
English; as, ‘he should be killed’ - हैं तव्य:. They denote that ‘the 
action or the state expressed by the root or derivative base, muet 
or ought to be done or undergone; as, वक्तब्य॑-वाच्यं-बचनीयं what 
ought to be said.’ The sense thus conveyed by them is that of 71763: , 
obligation or necessity; e. g.. ‘I have to gothere’ मया तत्र गन्तव्यं: 7 
must do it? मया AGHAST. 

6 156. These participles are used in sentences in the same 
way asthe passive of the roots from which they aro derived; ae, 
मद्वचनात्स राजा त्वयेदं वाच्यः ( R. XIV. 61 ) ‘the king should be told 
.this in my behalf’; अजा ग्रामं नेतव्या: ‘the sheep ought to be taker 
to the village; so असो दुहितुः पत्या परिग्रहपियमस्माभिः आवयितव्यः ( 5.7 ) 
‘ho should be made to hear the good fortune in the form of the

LESSON XV. 
Parr I. 
Indeclinable Past Participles or Gerunds. 
§ 158. The indeclinable past participle, in Sanskrit, commoniy 
| called ‘absolutive’ or ‘gerund,’ always denotes a prior action, or au 


^ action completed before another, and corresponds to the perfect parti- 
ciple, or the participle in tny having the senso of the perfect participle 


106 THE STUDENT'S GUIDE 


in English; as, प्रताहारी ससुपसृत्य सबिनयमत्रवीत (5.8) ‘the female 
door-keeper, drawing near or having drawn near, modestly said,’ बेशं- 
पायनो छहतामित्र ध्यात्वा सादरमत्रवीत ( K.18 ) "Vaisampáyana, as if 
contemplating for a moment, respectfully said.’ 

But in the sentence ‘going to a village, he touches a blade of 
grass on his way,’ we must say, ग्रामं गच्छन्‌ पथि qui स्पृशाति. 

3 159. Indeclinable past participlos in Sanskrit are formed 
by त्वा or by य (changed to त्य) when a preposition precedes a root 
( for rules, vide Dr. Kielhorn’s Grammar §§ 512-525). They are, 
ai before stated, used to denote a past or prior action, aud must have 
the same agent as the main verb; as, तुरासाहं पुरोधाय धाम स्वायंभुवं ययुः 
(Ku. II. 1) shaving placed Indra at their head (the gods) went to the 
abode of Brahman’. Hero the agent of ‘placing’ and ‘going’ is the 
same, and henee the gerund can be used; buta रतं हत्वा5हमागच्छम is 
wrong. In such «ases tho gerund cannot bo used; the Locative absolute 
construction will have to he used to express {thu same sense, as, 
तस्मिस्तन हते $हमागच्छम. So wa: पशुभिमिलित्या सिंहो विज्ञप्तः (H. 2) ‘the 
lion was entreated by a!] the beasts, having assembled together,’ 
स एनं दोषं प्रख्याप्य नगरान्निवास्यताम ( Mu. 1) ‘let him be expelled the 
city (by thee) having proclaimed this crime,’ 

3 160. The Sanvkrit gerunds aro very useful to economize the 
use of conjunctions and verbal forms in describing or narrating

Namul or Gerund in अम. 

§ 162. There is another kind of gerund in Sanskrit formed by 
the addition of अम्‌ immediately to the root or derivative base, and 
making the same changes as before the g of the passive Aorist (see 
Dr. Kielhorn’s Grammar § 526 ); as, Zrd ‘having thrown’ from fey; 
बाद ‘having spoken’; भोज ‘having dived’. 

S 163. When this gerund is repeated, it denotes a repetition 
or recurrence of the action or state expressed by the root, as, tate स्मारं 
नमाते शिवं (8. K.) ‘having often and often called to mind Siva, he 
hows to him,’ कलिंगनाथो मावि बद्धवेर इति आवं oid चंडवर्मा युद्धायोयतो बभूव 
(DX. IL. 3) ‘having repeatedly heard that the lord of the Kalifgas 
was hostilely inclined towards himself, Chaiidavarman became ready 
to fight’; so also पायं पायं, दृशं gst ‘having repeatedly drunk or seen.’

LESSON XVI. 
The Infinitive Mood. 

§ 172. When one action is represented as being done for 
another action, the latter is, in Sanskrit, expressed by the Infinitive, 
which is formed by the addition of the termination तुम to the root in 
the same way as the third person singular of the Periphrastic Future. 
Tt has the sense of ‘in order to,’ ‘for the purpose of,’ ‘for.’ and thus 
corresponds to that form of tho English Infinitive which is called 
‘infinitive of purpose’ or ‘gerund.’ The Sanskrit Infinitive has thus 
a dative sense, and may, if necessary, be replaced by the dative case 
of the verbal noun derived from the root; as, पारसीकांस्ततो जेतुं प्रतस्थे 
( R. IV. 60) ‘he then set out to conquer the Persians,’ i. e. ‘for the 
purpose of conquering &c.' Here Rg = जयाय, and the sentence may stand 
thus: पारसीकानां जयाय प्रतस्थेः 5० स्वेदसलिलस्नाताऽपि पुनः खातुमवातरम 


(E. 147) where स्नार्तु = स्नानाय. 
Obs. (a) Like the English infinitive, the Sanskrit Infinitive is 


a remnant of an old inflexion. In Vedic times the verbal noun formed 
from a root by the termiuation तु (गंतु, यातु) was regularly declined. 
We find such forme as std, गंतवे, मंतोः, as if vig was a regular noun. In 
course of time the use of the forms गंतोः, wea, became less and less 
frequent, and the form that was chiefly used was the accusative in- 
flection. It was subsequently considered to have a dative sense, and 
hence the present Infinitive form in Sanskrit has always the sense 


of the dative. 
(b) The Sanskrit Infinitive corresponds in Latin to the Supine 


in um (datum = दातुं) which is properly the accusative of a verbal 
substantive, just as in Sanskrit; ‘Themistocles Argos Aabitatum 
(स्थातुं) concessit’ "Themistocles retired to lipe at Argos. The same 
sense is expressed, as in Sanskrit, by the dative of the gerundive 
participle; ‘Decemviri legibus scribundis’ (बिधिनिमाणाय ) ‘Decemvirs 
for framing Jaws.’ 

§ 173. It follows from the definition that the Infinitive in 
Sanskrit cannot stand as the subject or object of a verb. It has no 


! 


TO SANSKRIT COMPOSITION, 115 


connection with any words in the sentence, except that it may, where 
: possible, govern a noun in the same case as the root from which it is 
derived. Where the Infinitive occurs in English as the subject or object 
of a verb an abstract noun from the root must be used in Sanskrit ; 
as, ‘to get up early in the morning is wholesome’ matę उत्थान (not 
उत्थातुं ) आरोग्यावहं; '1 learn to sing’ अहं गानमधीये. 

(e) The Infinitive after verbs of ‘seeing,’ ‘hearing,’ is, as in Latin, 
translated by the present participle; as ‘I heard him speak’ भाषमाणं 
ammé; so अधीयानं gaat d ‘He saw him study 


§ 174. The strict sense of the Sanskrit Infinitive is motste or 
purpose of an action ; but there are some cases. as in English, where 
the Infinitivo is used with nouns and also adjectives; as, ‘fit to go.” 
“able to go,’ ‘time to read.’ Such cases are, however, limited by Sanskrit 
idiom. Some of the principal cases of this nature are given below. 


§ 175. *The Infinitive is used with verbs and substantives 
meaning to wish’ or ‘desire.’ provided the agents of the infinitive 
and the verb are same; as पिनाकपाणि पतिमाप्तुमिच्छति ( Ku. V. 53) 
‘desires to obtain for her husband the Pinaka-handed god ( Siva / 
50 WY वाञ्छति शांभवों गणपतेराखुं क्षधातः फणी ( P. 1.3); but not md 
गंतुमहमिच्छामे '1 wish thee to go’, where the agents of गम and EN ure

$179. Tho Sanskrit ‘Infinitive has no passive form, the same

the object of the Infinitive and the verb is the same, it is. in the passive 
voice. put in the nominative ease, leaving it to be understood with the 
Infinitive; as ग्रन्थं पठितुमिच्छाति: तेन ग्रन्थः पठितुमिष्यते, the object of 
पठितुं being d, if necossary. In this ease it will not do to say aped 
पठितुमिष्यते, for this would be an impersonal construction, though gw 
is not an intransitive verb.

LESSON XVII, 


Tenses and Moods. 


$182. In Sanskrit there are altogether ten Tenses and Moods: 
Present, Imperfect, Perfect, Aorist, Periphrastic Future, Simple 
Future, Imperative, Potential, Conditional and Benedictive. The ten 
lakaras given by Panini arc—az , we, लिट , FE, लद , we, लोट , लियः, 
we, and लेटू.* Of these the last ( लेट ) is found only in the Veda, and 
has the sense of the subjunctive, and it is generally termed ‘the Vedic 
subjunctive.’ The remaining nine respectively correspond to the 
tenses and moods given above, the Benedictive being in Sanskrit 
included in ल and distinguished from the Potential ( ARRE ) १: 
आशीछिङ. 

8183. Every verb in Sanskrit. whether in a primitive, causa] 
desiderative. or frequentative form, may be conjugated in the ten 
tenses and moods, though the last two derivative forms of verbs are 
very rarely used except in the Present tense. The senses conveved 
by them are usually expressed by other forms or combinations of 


words ; as जिगमिषति = गन्तुमिच्छति; अटाट्यत = भूशमटति. 


8184. Some of the tenses and moods in Sanskrit exactly 
correspond to the tenses and moods in other languages, and some are 
peculiar to Sanskrit. In this and the next three Lessons are given 
their uses and meanings. The Present tense and the Imperative and 
Benedictive moods are considered in this Lesson. 





*This terminology of Panini is artificial, and is not based on auy 
particular principle. Other grammarians have adopted a some- 
what rational nomenclature. The names of the several tenses and 
moods, according to them, are in the above order as follows:—weaeaf 
( वर्तमाना ), ह्यस्तनी, परोक्षा, अद्यतनी, श्वस्तनी, भविष्यन्ती, पंचमी सप्तमी (these 
two alone being quite artificial), क्रियातिपत्तिः and आज्ञीः The feminine 
is used, because the word g7@: is understood after each,

Present Tense. 


8185. The Present tense is used to denote au action taking place, 
or a fact existing, at tho Present time; as, जगतः पितरो बन्दे (1. 1. 1) 
‘ E salute the parents of the Universe.’ 

Obs. Strictly speaking, the present tense iu Sanskrit correspond? 
to the present progressive. imperfect or incomplete form, which ex- 
presses the continuance of an action which és begun. Patatjali says 'प्रव- 
त्स्याविरामं शासितव्या भवन्ती which indicates that the action denoted by 
a verb in the Present tense 13 yet continuing and had not stopped; us 
बहति जलामेयं fate गंधानियं ( Mn. 1) ‘this lady brings (is bringing ) 
water; this (another) grinds (is grinding ) odorous substances’; एता- 
स्तपस्तिकन्यका इत एवाभिवतन्ते ( §. 1 ) ‘these ascetic-girls come ( are con- 
ing ) in this very direction. There is no distinct form in Sanskrit 
to expres» this continuous action ; hence its usual signification. 


[t must. however. be remembered that it Is only by a special 
adverb or by the context, that the Present tense can be confined to 
mean a present act solely; as देवदत्तो गच्छति (1.० अघुना ); or संप्रत्यधी 
atag. The principal use of the Present indefinite is. as observed by 
Buiu ( Grammar p. 185 )* to express what ts true at all. times.’ It 
expresse: present time only as representing all time. The permanent 
arrangements and laws of nature, the peculiarities and propensities 
of living beings, and whatever is constant. regular. aud uniform, have 
to he represented by the present indefinit: as सत्संगतिः कथय किंन 
करोति पुसां ( Bh. 11. 23) * say what the company of the good does not 
de tomma: अस्त्युत्तरस्यां विशि हिमालयो नाम amfa: (Ku 1. 1 ) 

there is (stands ) in the northern direction the lord of mountains 
alled Himálaya'; so नास्ति जीबितादन्यदाभिमततरामेह wasiqai ( K. 35. ); 
ऋषीणां पुनराद्यानां वाचमर्था$नुधावति (0. 1); न खल बहिरुपाधीन्प्रतिय 
मन्यन्ते ( Mal. 1 ) &e.

LESSON XVII. 
The Potential Mood. 

§ 196. The Potential mood in Sanskrit corresponds to the 
Subjunctive mood in English and Latin, but it has uot all the senses 
and uses of the English Subjunctive, nor the wide application of the 
Latin Subjunctive. In English the Subjunctive mood is not used 
in independent clauses; in Latin, it is used without any preceding 
verb to express à te$2À, but it is generally used in dependent assertions; 
but in Sanskrit the Potential mood is used both in independent and 
dependent asscrtions; नीचेराख्य गिरिमधिवसेः (Me. 26); कृत्यं घटेत BEAT 
यदि deed स्यात (Mål 1). We shall now see in what senses it is 
used in Sanskrit. 

& 197. The Potential expresses (A) probability, command, wish. 
prayer. hope. and capability; (D) itis used iu dependent clauses in 
which the above senses are Implied; and (C) itis used in condition or 
hypothetical sentences, in which one statement depends upon another 
as its reason, or condition. 

A. 

६ 198. The senses of ‘probability,’ ‘command’ &c. exprossed by 
the Potential are expressed, in English, by ‘may’, ‘shall’, or ‘should,’ 
aud often by ‘wsll’, ‘would,’ ‘could,’ ‘might, as used in direct assertions; 
as. लभत सिकताख तेलमापे यत्नतः पीडयन (Dh. IT. 5) ‘one may even get oil 
from sand, by diligently squeezing it together’; atg भूषणविक्रयं नरपतौ 
को नाम संभावयेत (Mu. 5) *who, indeed, would think it probable 
that the Maurya king would sell ornaments? १ जेतारं कार्तिफेयरप

LESSON XIX. 
Imperfect, Perfect, and Aorist. 


§ 207. ‘In English there is only one tense referring to past time: 
it is the past indefinite or Aorist? ( Howard's -Vote on English Verb, P. 
12 ); as, ‘I walked.’ In Sanskrit there are three tenses referring to & 
past time: Imperfect, Perfect and Aorist. Each of these had originally 
a peculiar signification. In ancient works, or works composed at a 
time when, we have reason to believe, Sanskrit was a spoken lan- 
guage. they aro found to be used in their exact senses: later on, as 
Sanskrit became less and less a spoken language, writers began to 
use these three tenses promiscnously. The serses in which they were 
originally used are as follows :— 

The Imperfect is, according to Panini. अनयतने ga , ४. ¢. it denotes 
past action done previous to the current day. hence at a time removed 
from this day. Tho Perfect is परोक्षे लिट . १. ०. it denotes past action 
done previous to this day. and which was not witnessed by the speak- 
„er. The Aorist is merely wem gg | ४. €. it has reference to a past 
time indefinitely or generally, without reference to any particular time. 
An action done before to-day is expressed bv the Perfect or Imperfect; 
what remains for the Aorist is, therefore, to express & past action done 


136 THE STUDENT'S GUIDE 


very recently, say. iu tho course of the eurrent duy. or having reference 
to a present act. The Aorist. therefore, merely implies the completion 
of an action at a past time generally. aud also an action done at a very 
recent time, as during the course of this day. The Imperfect and 
Perfect are used in narrating events of past occurrence, generally in 
remote past time; the Aorist is uscd in dialogues and conversations 
which refer to recent past actions; but itis not used to denote past 
specified time. or to narrate events.* Thus in the whole of the Purusha- 
Sükta ( Rigveda X. 90) the Imperfect or Perfect alone is used. the 
events narrated referring to a past time, and in the Aitareya-Brahmana 
recent actions are shown bv the Aorist: as. स भरमि विश्वतो दत्वा अत्यतिष्ठ- 
ZIG, गावो ह जज्ञिरे तस्मात 6G अजानि ते वे wal यजस्व मामनेनेति. But 
later Sanskrit writers lost sight of this difference between the Imperfect, 
Perfect, and Aorist. ond the three are found to be promiscuously used 
to denote merely a past action, whether recent, remote or not wit- 
nessed by the speaker; o- तदाहं किमकरवं क्रागमं कि व्यलपमिति सर्वमेव 
नाज्ञासिषम ( K. 166 ). 

§ 208. The Imperfect. besides its general use, is sometimes 
used to ask a question referring to a recent time; as. अगच्छ स्कि स ara 
` has he gone to the village 7°. but when a remote time is implied, the 
Perfect alone should he used; as. कंस जघान कि ( 5. K.) edid he kill 
Kamsa 7

some distraction or unconscious state of the mind; it should not, there- 
fore. be used in the first person, except in this sense; as. बहु ज गद्‌ gt-

LESSON XX. 
The two Futures and the Conditional. 

§ 219. In English futurity is expressed by will or shall; in 
Sanskrit there are two different tensea to denote a future action; the 
First or Periphrastic Future. and the Second or Simple Future. 
The original distinction between the two is nearly the same as between 
Imperfect and Aorist, except this, that the latter refers to a past time, 


-the former to a future one; in other words, the First Future denotes. 


an action which is not to take place in the course of the current day, 
while the Second Future refers to a future time generally or indeti- 
nitely, as also to a recent future action. Thus the First Future denotes. 


140 THE STUDENT'S GUIDE 


& remote fuiure time not of to-day; while the Second Future is 
employed to denote indefinite future time, to-day's future time, and 
recent and future continuous time; as, पैचषेरहोभिर्वयमभव तत्र stare: (Mu. 
5) ‘we ourselves shall go there iu five or six days; एते...उन्मूलितारः 
कपिकेतनेन (Ki. III 22) -they will be extirpated by Arjuna (the 
monkey-baunered )'; यास्यत्वय शकुंतला (5. 4) -Sakuntala will depart 
(departs) to-day’: साविष्यन्ते नयनसुभगं खे भवन्तं बलाकाः ( Me. 9) ‘cranes 
will wait (at some future time) upon thee. lovely to behold, in the 
sky.’ Writers are not so loose in the use of these two tenses, as in the 
mse of the three past tenses. The Poriphrastic Future is far less 
frequently used; and where it is used, it gensrally denotes a remote 
(not to-day’s ) future action; while the Simple Future is used to 
denote any indefinite future action, 


§ 213. When the close proximity of future action is intended, 
.the Present or the Future may be used; as कदा गमिष्यसि-एष गच्छासि 
गमिष्पामि वा (£. K.) ‘when will you go? I shall just go.’ 

§ 214. *Wheu hope is expressed in a conditional form. the 
Aorist, the Present. or Simple Future is used in both the clauses to 
denote a future time: as. देवश्वेद्यषींदू वर्षति वर्षिष्यति बा धान्यमवाप्स्स वपामों 
qurar वा (5. 1९. ) 111६ were to rain we should sow corn.’ 

६ 215. Sometimes the Simple Future is used iu the sense of 
the Imperative as a courteous way of telling one to do a thing; as’ 
तदा मम पाशांइठेत्स्यसि (H. 1.) afterwards cut my bonds’; so पश्चात्सरः 
प्राति गमिष्यसि मानसं तत ( V. 4); this corresponds to the polite form of 
expression in English: as, ‘you will see me at the station to-morrow at 
12 noon.’ 


§ 216. The Conditional is used in those conditional sentences 
in which the non-performance of the’ action is implied, or ‘where the 
falsity of the antecedent is implied as a matter of fact,’ It usually 
corresponds to the English pluperfect Conditional, and must in

N. B.—The Sanskrit Conditional should not be used in those con- 
ditional sentences, where it is merely implied that under a supposed 
condition, such a consequence would follow; as, ‘if he were here, he 
would defend his country bravely; ‘if I could agree to this your plan 
I would rather die than live? In translating such sentences the 


Potential is used; as, यदि सोऽत्र संनिहितो भवेत्ताह स्वदेशं वीरवद्रक्षेत. 


Additional Remarks on the use of 
the Tenses and Moods. 


8 217. The intricacies and details of thc several forms of the 
Present, Past and Future are not found in Sanskrit. There is one 
principal tense, and the different forms are usually expressed by that 
tense. Evenin English forms like the future progressive passive, 
future progressive passive continuous. are of a modern growth. and: 
are not of frequent occurrence. Hence it is that a student of Sanskrit 
generally finds it difficult to translate the multifarious forms of 
these tenses into their corresponding Sonskrit equivalents. Some hints 
on this point are given in the following sections, stating in more detail 
what has been giveu iu the three preceding lessons. 


Present, Past and Future. 


§ 218. As before observed, the Present tense in its simple form 

( ealloed the present indefinite) has in Sanskrit most of the senses 
Which it has in English ( 8 186 ). The English Past tense has, accord- 
ing to the usage of classical authors at least, come to be expressed by 
any of the three tenses referring to a past action, and future time: 


142 THE STUDENT'8 GUIDE 


is generally denoted by the two Future forms in Sanskrit, and some- 
times by the Potential mood ( 8 198 ). But the several forms of the 
different tenses are not considered by Sanskrit writers, if they are to 
be translated into Sanskrit, other forms have to be used for them, 


8 219. The continuous forms, called ‘present continuous’, ‘ past 
continuous,’ and ‘future continuous; may be generally translated 
into Sanskrit by merely putting the simple forms of the tenses; as, 
the +s studying his lesson’ स पाठमधीते, and not अधीवानोऽस्ति: for the 
progressive or continuous form is a true or strict present tense ( Bain’s 
Grammar P. 186); ‘the boys are now playing’ बालका अघुना क्रीडन्ति; 
‘the sun was shining’ रविरतपत ( not तपन आसीत्‌ ); he will be prepar- 
ing his lesson’ स पाठमध्येष्यते. 

Obs,—' The present participle with आम is used in those cases 
where a regular continuity of action is intended to be expressed, such 
as is stated in $8145. When these continuous forms occur iu sul- 
ordinat» sentences. the locative absolute of the present participle may 
be conveniently used; as. ‘while the minister was speaking. a messenger 
entered the assembly,’ भाषमाणेऽमात्ये faga: सभां प्राविशत. 


8 220. The emphatic forms, which exist only for the Present 
and Past, may be translated by एव, नूनं, We or some such word ex- 
pressing certainty. with the simple forms; us. ‘1 do consider thee 
guilty’ अहे त्वामपराधिनं भन्ये खल--एव, or नूनं त्वां... मन्ये; ‘he did tell a

Should and Would. 


६ 231. Should expressing contingent futurity, obligation or 
duty, is translated by the Potential mood (§ 198), or by the potential

(a) After words expressing number अपि has the sense of 
-totality’; as, सर्वेरपि राज्ञां प्रयोजनं (P. 1.1) ‘kings have need of all 
( not excluding even one )’; 80 चतुर्णामपि वर्णानाम. 

(b) Joined to interrogative pronouns and their derivatives, it 
has the sense of ‘any’, and sometimes that of ‘indescribable’; see § 135. 

(c) यद्यापे--तथापि are correlatives, and mean ‘though—yet, still 
or nevertheless.’ 

§ 248. *अयि is used (1) as a gentle address in the sense of 
‘friend, prythee’; as, आयि विवेकविभ्रांताभिहितं ( M. 1) ‘friend, you have 
said something void of judgement’, अयि प्रातर्देवयननसंभवे देवि साते (U. 4) 
‘O darling Sita, born from the sacrifice to the gods’; (2) In asking 
a gentle question; अयि जौबितनाथ जीवासे ( Ka. IV. 3) ‘lord of my life, 
art thou alive ?' 

§ 249. अये is principally used to express (1) ‘surprise’, 
wonder’; अये भगषत्यरुन्धती ( U. 5 ) ‘Oh! itis the revered Arundhati; 
80 अये मय्येव भुकुटीघरः Ham: (U.5); (2) ‘grief’, ‘dejection’, ‘fear’; 
अये वेवपादप्मोपजीषिनोवस्थेयम ( Mu. 2 ) ‘alas! this is the state of a 
servant of the lotus-like feet of His Majesty’. 

§ 250. † अहह is used to denote (1) joy, astonishment, or

has the sense of * a little, somewhat,’ and is equivalent to the English 
ssh in ‘blackish’ &c. It is prefixed to adjectives ; as, आपिंगल ‘a little 
tawany’ ; आमत्तानां कोकिलानां कृजितेः (M.3 ) ‘with the warblings of 
cuckoos somewhat drunk with passion’,

sometimes, merely as an expletive ; as, आ wa मन्यसे (G. M. ).

A wicked person is not to be trusted because he speaks sweet 
words.

be acquainted with the learned men of the city. 

He, hastily coming up to me, as if enraged. said that I had 
greatly offended him. 

That misfortunes never come singly 15 a wise saving often realized 
by men in this world. 

When the enemies fell upon us like so many hailstones, we did 
not know what to do. 

N ot*heing fed with food for a long time, he became, as it were, 
ready to die. 

The whole world considers me to be powerless because I do not 
harm anybody. 

Immediately on hearing my words, the rash man, with only one 
attendaut, became ready to run the risk. |
